Dengue in Children’s Brains: Pakistani Radiologists Map the Virus’s Imaging Fingerprints - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A team of radiologists at Aga Khan University Hospital in Karachi, Pakistan, has compiled a systematic pictorial review of the imaging features of pediatric dengue, published in Pediatric Radiology. The study categorizes these findings into three mechanistic groups: direct viral invasion, immune-mediated injury, and reversible edema syndromes. This classification is crucial for guiding clinical management, as each category has distinct prognoses and treatment implications. The research highlights that dengue can cause severe neurological complications, including encephalitis, spinal cord inflammation, and paralysis in children. It also identifies specific imaging patterns, such as the double doughnut sign and Jack-o’-lantern sign, which are associated with severe dengue encephalitis and rhombencephalitis, respectively. These signs are important for early diagnosis and intervention. The study underscores the need for radiologists to recognize these patterns to provide appropriate care, as some conditions may be reversible with supportive treatment. Pakistan has experienced repeated dengue outbreaks, including a major epidemic in 2022, which highlights the relevance of this research in dengue-endemic regions.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Tuesday, October 6, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer Dengue in Children’s Brains: Pakistani Radiologists Map the Virus’s Imaging Fingerprints by October 6, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Dengue in Children's Brains: Pakistani Radiologists Map the Virus's Imaging Fingerprints Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Dengue fever has long been thought of as a disease of high fever, aching joints, and, in its severe forms, dangerous plasma leakage and hemorrhage. Yet in recent years clinicians across the tropical world have become increasingly aware that the dengue virus can also attack the nervous system, producing encephalitis, spinal cord inflammation, and even paralysis in children. A team of radiologists at Aga Khan University Hospital in Karachi, Pakistan, has now compiled a systematic pictorial review of what pediatric dengue looks like on magnetic resonance imaging, organizing the findings by the underlying disease mechanisms that produce them. The review, published in Pediatric Radiology, is intended as a practical diagnostic atlas for physicians working in dengue-endemic regions, where recognizing these patterns quickly can change the course of a child’s illness. Pakistan has experienced repeated large dengue outbreaks, including a major epidemic in 2022 that prompted the World Health Organization to issue a formal disease outbreak notice. In such settings, children presenting with fever and new neurological symptoms pose a difficult diagnostic problem. The symptoms of dengue-related brain involvement, ranging from seizures and altered consciousness to weakness and confusion, overlap heavily with those of other viral encephalitides, autoimmune conditions, and metabolic derangements. Magnetic resonance imaging is often the decisive tool, but only if radiologists know which patterns to look for and how to distinguish them from close mimics. The Karachi group, led by Kumail Khandwala and Kiran Hilal, set out to fill that gap by illustrating the full spectrum of dengue-related neuroradiological findings in children and correlating each pattern with the published literature. The review’s central organizing principle is pathophysiology. Rather than listing findings haphazardly, the authors divide the imaging manifestations of pediatric dengue into three broad mechanistic categories: direct viral invasion of nervous tissue, immune-mediated injury, and reversible edema syndromes. This framework matters because the categories carry different prognoses and demand different clinical responses. Direct viral invasion tends to produce the most severe imaging abnormalities and the gravest illness, while the reversible edema patterns, as their name implies, often resolve completely with supportive care. Immune-mediated disease occupies an intermediate position, sometimes responding dramatically to immunomodulatory treatment. Understanding which category a given scan belongs to is therefore not an academic exercise but a practical step toward appropriate management. Discover more Check Symptoms Book Lab Tests Study Anatomy Try Brain Games Study Biology Within the direct-invasion category, the review describes meningoencephalitis, involvement of the deep gray matter nuclei, and rhombencephalitis, which is inflammation centered on the brainstem. The most striking and diagnostically valuable findings in this group are two named signs that have become associated with severe dengue encephalitis. The first is the so-called double doughnut sign, in which symmetric lesions in the thalami develop a central area of necrosis surrounded by a ring of edema, producing a pattern on axial images that resembles two doughnuts side by side. This appearance reflects acute necrotizing encephalopathy, a fulminant form of viral brain injury in which the damage is driven partly by a cytokine storm rather than by the virus alone. The review notes that dengue-associated acute necrotizing encephalopathy is now considered by many investigators to be a true variant of that syndrome rather than a mere mimic, a conclusion supported by a recent systematic review of published cases. The second named pattern is the Jack-o’-lantern sign, seen in dengue rhombencephalitis. On imaging, the brainstem shows scattered, patchy areas of abnormal signal within the pons that give the structure a carved, hollowed-out appearance reminiscent of a Halloween pumpkin. Brainstem involvement in dengue is uncommon but clinically dangerous, because the pons and medulla control breathing, heart rate, and other vital functions. The review highlights that this sign has been reported to be reversible in some patients who survive the acute illness, which underscores the importance of recognizing it early and providing aggressive supportive care rather than assuming a uniformly fatal course. Distinguishing dengue rhombencephalitis from other causes of brainstem inflammation, such as Listeria infection or other flaviviruses, relies on the clinical context, serological testing, and the specific distribution of lesions. Discover more Find Treatments Find Family Lawyer Children's The immune-mediated category encompasses two related but distinct white matter disorders. The first is acute leukoencephalopathy with restricted diffusion, a condition in which the brain’s white matter shows marked restriction of water molecule movement on diffusion-weighted imaging, indicating acute cytotoxic injury. The second is acute disseminated encephalomyelitis, a post-infectious autoimmune demyelinating syndrome in which the body’s immune response, triggered by the virus, mistakenly attacks the myelin sheaths of its own neurons. The review emphasizes that these two entities can be told apart by their diffusion characteristics and their timing relative to the infection. Acute leukoencephalopathy with restricted diffusion typically appears during the acute phase and shows true restricted diffusion, whereas acute disseminated encephalomyelitis tends to emerge days to weeks after the febrile illness and shows different diffusion behavior along with lesions that may enhance after contrast administration. This distinction has direct therapeutic consequences, because the autoimmune condition is the one that typically warrants corticosteroids or other immunotherapy. The third category, reversible edema syndromes, includes two entities with reassuring outcomes when properly identified. Reversible splenial lesion syndrome involves a characteristic lesion in the splenium, the posterior portion of the corpus callosum that connects the brain’s two hemispheres. On MRI the lesion appears as a small, round area of restricted diffusion, and, crucially, it typically disappears completely on follow-up imaging as the child recovers. Dengue has been reported as a cause of this syndrome in multiple case reports from Asia and elsewhere. The second, less commonly reported entity in the dengue context is posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome, a condition in which the parietal and occipital regions of the brain develop vasogenic edema, often in association with severe hypertension, seizures, and visual disturbances. The Karachi group has previously studied pediatric posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome in depth, examining how blood pressure relates to imaging severity and atypical MRI features, and that expertise informs the current review’s treatment of the dengue-associated form. Beyond the brain, the review extends its atlas to the spinal cord and peripheral nerves. Dengue-associated myelitis, inflammation of the spinal cord, can produce transverse myelitis with weakness and sensory loss below the affected level, and MRI plays a central role in defining the extent and character of the cord lesion. Guillain-Barré syndrome, the autoimmune peripheral neuropathy that causes ascending weakness and can progress to respiratory failure, has also been increasingly linked to dengue infection, with recent systematic reviews cataloging the clinical characteristics, outcomes, and predictors of severity in these patients. The review stresses that MRI is valuable in differentiating dengue-related myeloradiculopathies from other infectious and inflammatory causes, a distinction that determines whether antiviral, immunomodulatory, or purely supportive strategies are most appropriate. Perhaps the most practically useful single insight in the review concerns hemorrhage. The authors highlight that intralesional hemorrhagic changes, detected on susceptibility-weighted imaging or gradient recalled echo sequences, particularly within the deep gray nuclei and the brainstem, represent a valuable marker that favors dengue over several viral and inflammatory mimickers. Susceptibility-weighted sequences are exquisitely sensitive to blood breakdown products, and their ability to reveal microhemorrhages in characteristic locations gives radiologists an additional clue that can tip the diagnostic balance in dengue’s favor. In a region where Japanese encephalitis, tuberculosis, and other neurotropic infections circulate alongside dengue, such pattern-recognition tools can meaningfully shorten the path to the correct diagnosis. The broader significance of the Karachi review lies in its synthesis. By systematically illustrating the full range of pediatric dengue neuroradiology and anchoring each pattern in its pathophysiological mechanism, the authors aim to improve diagnostic confidence among radiologists and clinicians who may encounter only a handful of such cases in a career. As climate change expands the geographic range of the Aedes mosquito vectors and dengue incidence rises globally, the neurological complications of the virus will increasingly be seen outside traditional endemic zones, in emergency departments where familiarity with these imaging signatures is limited. A pattern-based approach, grounded in the double doughnut and Jack-o’-lantern signs, the diffusion characteristics that separate leukoencephalopathy from demyelination, and the hemorrhagic markers that distinguish dengue from its mimics, offers a practical framework for timely recognition and management of one of the more feared complications of a rapidly spreading tropical disease. 佛罗里达州面临数十年来最大规模的登革热 暴发,当地感染人数超过 250 例 - CubaHeadlines

Florida Faces Largest Dengue Outbreak in Decades with Over 250 Local Infections - CubaHeadlines
新闻简报Florida is facing its largest dengue outbreak in over eight decades, with more than 250 local infections confirmed by week 38 of 2026. The outbreak, which began in July, has shifted its epicenter to Hillsborough County, particularly in areas like South Tampa, Davis Islands, and Harbor Island. This marks a significant change from previous outbreaks centered in southern Florida. The outbreak has seen a rise in local transmission cases, surpassing the previous record of fewer than 200 infections in 2023. Notably, the first dengue-related death in 2026 occurred on September 4 in Tampa, highlighting the severity of the situation. Authorities have declared states of emergency in Hillsborough, Pasco, and Pinellas counties, and fumigation efforts are underway to control mosquito populations. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has provided $800,000 in assistance, but Florida has not formally requested federal involvement. Public health experts warn that the lack of immunity among residents in the Tampa area increases their vulnerability to the virus. The outbreak has raised concerns about the potential for severe dengue forms, especially given the circulation of all four serotypes in Cuba, which is also experiencing a significant dengue surge. Travelers from Cuba and other Caribbean regions are advised to take precautions such as using EPA-registered repellents and eliminating standing water to reduce mosquito breeding sites.
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 于 2026 年 7 月开始引起关注,并在全年不断升级,2026 年 9 月 4 日报告了第一例死亡病例。9 月下旬宣布紧急状态,并将希尔斯伯勒县的紧急状态延长至 2026 年 10 月 8 日。
地点与对象
暴发 以希尔斯伯勒县为中心,特别是南坦帕、戴维斯群岛和哈伯岛等地区。它影响了佛罗里达州的当地居民,特别是坦帕地区。
事件
佛罗里达州正在经历登革热 暴发,到 2026 年第 38 周,已确认超过 250 例当地感染病例。这是美国大陆 8 年来最大规模的 暴发。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致 252 例当地 传播 病例,超过了之前 2023 年感染人数不足 200 例的记录。2026 年 9 月 4 日,坦帕报告了 1 例死亡。暴发 已从传统热点地区转移到希尔斯伯勒县,表明当地存在严重传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
希尔斯伯勒、帕斯科和皮尼拉斯县的官员已宣布当地进入紧急状态。灭蚊队正在积极工作以减少蚊虫数量。疾控中心已提供800,000美元的援助,但佛罗里达州尚未请求联邦介入。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Florida Faces Largest Dengue Outbreak in Decades with Over 250 Local Infections - CubaHeadlines

News Brief
Florida is facing its largest dengue outbreak in over eight decades, with more than 250 local infections confirmed by week 38 of 2026. The outbreak, which began in July, has shifted its epicenter to Hillsborough County, particularly in areas like South Tampa, Davis Islands, and Harbor Island. This marks a significant change from previous outbreaks centered in southern Florida. The outbreak has seen a rise in local transmission cases, surpassing the previous record of fewer than 200 infections in 2023. Notably, the first dengue-related death in 2026 occurred on September 4 in Tampa, highlighting the severity of the situation. Authorities have declared states of emergency in Hillsborough, Pasco, and Pinellas counties, and fumigation efforts are underway to control mosquito populations. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has provided $800,000 in assistance, but Florida has not formally requested federal involvement. Public health experts warn that the lack of immunity among residents in the Tampa area increases their vulnerability to the virus. The outbreak has raised concerns about the potential for severe dengue forms, especially given the circulation of all four serotypes in Cuba, which is also experiencing a significant dengue surge. Travelers from Cuba and other Caribbean regions are advised to take precautions such as using EPA-registered repellents and eliminating standing water to reduce mosquito breeding sites.
Original source text (English)
CubaHeadlines Florida Faces Largest Dengue Outbreak in Decades with Over 250 Local Infections Saturday, October 3, 2026 by Christopher Ramirez Florida Beach (Reference Image) - Image © CiberCuba In 2026, Florida is grappling with an unprecedented dengue outbreak, marking the largest in the mainland United States in over eight decades. According to the Florida Department of Health's arbovirus report, more than 250 local transmission cases have been confirmed by week 38 of the year. This alarming number surpasses the state's previous record, set in 2023, which saw fewer than 200 local infections. Florida now finds itself in uncharted territory, facing a scenario not seen since the 1940s. Unexpected Epicenter: Hillsborough County Unlike past outbreaks centered in southern Florida, this one has its epicenter in Hillsborough County, home to Tampa. The county accounts for 223 of the more than 250 cases reported statewide, shifting the focus away from traditional hotspots. For the first time this year, local transmission cases (252) have outnumbered imported ones (245), indicating that the virus is spreading independently within the local population rather than being introduced by travelers from endemic areas. Emergency Declarations and Response In late September, authorities in Hillsborough, Pasco, and Pinellas counties declared local states of emergency. Hillsborough extended its emergency status until October 8. To curb the spread, fumigation teams are working tirelessly in neighborhoods like South Tampa, Davis Islands, and Harbor Island to reduce mosquito populations. The outbreak has claimed its first victim: an 80-year-old woman who died in Tampa on September 4, marking the first dengue-related death in Florida in 2026. The outbreak, which began gaining attention in July, has continued to escalate ever since. Public Health Concerns and Regional Impact Dawn Wesson, a dengue expert from Tulane University, warns that most residents in the Tampa area lack immunity to any form of the virus, making them particularly susceptible. She also suggests that local travel patterns may partially explain why the outbreak shifted to Tampa. Wesson pointed out the noticeable absence of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) in the outbreak response. Florida has not requested the federal agency's involvement, although the CDC has provided $800,000 in assistance to the state health department. Challenges for Cuban Travelers and Broader Context The situation poses additional challenges for Cubans in southern Florida who frequently travel to the island. In 2026, Cuba is estimated to have around 7,133 dengue cases, a figure 7.4 times higher than expected for this time of year, according to the Global Dengue Observatory. The Cuban regime is also contending with the circulation of all four virus serotypes (DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3, and DENV-4), increasing the risk of severe hemorrhagic forms amid a healthcare system collapse characterized by a shortage of fumigants, accumulating garbage, and lack of clean water. Additionally, chikungunya, which re-emerged in July 2025, continues to circulate in 2026, as warned by the Ministry of Public Health. Precautionary Measures for Travelers Travelers returning from Cuba or other Caribbean areas should take extra precautions: use EPA-registered repellents containing DEET, wear long-sleeved clothing, stay in air-conditioned or screened-in spaces, and eliminate any standing water around the home, including in bottle caps or tires. The Aedes aegypti mosquito, the primary vector for dengue, can bite at any time of day but is most active at dawn and dusk. Florida is also considering allowing pharmacists to administer the chikungunya vaccine (VIMKUNYA, approved for those aged 12 and older), although this measure is not yet fully in place. There is currently no dengue vaccine recommended for the general public in the mainland U.S. Wesson somberly noted the potential long-term implications: "There's a good chance this is the new normal." Frequently Asked Questions about the Dengue Outbreak in Florida What is causing the dengue outbreak in Florida? The outbreak is primarily driven by local transmission in Hillsborough County, with the virus spreading independently of travelers from endemic regions. How are authorities responding to the dengue outbreak in Florida? Local states of emergency have been declared, and extensive fumigation efforts are underway to control mosquito populations. What precautions should travelers take regarding dengue? Travelers should use EPA-registered repellents, wear protective clothing, stay in screened areas, and remove standing water to reduce mosquito habitats. News Brief
In Broward County, Florida, officials confirmed a locally-acquired case of dengue fever, marking the first such instance in the county. This case is part of a larger outbreak in Florida, which the authorities describe as one of the largest in the continental United States. As of the week of September 2026, the CDC reported 485 cases of dengue fever in Florida in 2026, with nearly half acquired locally across eight counties. The Florida Department of Health in Broward noted that the county had already reported 20 travel-related cases this year. The outbreak is under investigation, and no specific risk level or detailed response measures have been outlined in the supplied evidence. The confirmation of the locally-acquired case was made by the Florida Department of Health, and the CDC provided the total case count. The information is based on the content of the news report, which was published on October 6, 2026, on Facebook.
